He had been told about a rock, and a knife, and a boy who lit a thing no bigger than a barley grain and walked away from it. He had expected as much, since those five fought as he would have had his own fight, with nothing showing. Then the factor told him the rest.

Eight had come down that road at a run, and had stopped in the air.

He had made the factor say it twice. The old man with the stick had stepped out, and struck the ice once, and there had been a thing across the cut from one bank to the other, as high as the scree. Eight hounds broke on it. It stood for exactly as long as the man never lifted a foot; and when it went, he went down with it, like a tent with the pole drawn.

Teague had never seen an Iron Wall. He had heard of them, as a man who works the holds hears of what is kept behind the walls of towns. They belonged to tribunals, and a person did not come by one in a meadow. Whoever carried such a thing up a border road on a stick had been somebody, a long way from here, and had left off being it; and Teague was not such a fool as to ask what.

He had asked something else, and it was what a captain needed to know. Could the man move?

He could not. That was the whole of it, and Teague had carried it home down a moor, turning it over.

The Force went out along a line and was done. He had been paid for that for the better part of his life, and it had made him first on a sheet of sailcloth: he could put his palm to a thing that six men could not shift, and it shifted. It was a thing for roads: a block came down on a ledge, or a wheel went into a ditch, or a gate would not open; and he arrived, and it was settled, and the wrist kept the account.

A wall did not arrive. It stayed. It could not come to the trouble; the trouble had to be brought to it, and whoever stood behind it had to be worth the bringing. He had seen that much from a factor's telling; the boy with the book had seen it from two feet away, and had gone and stood there.

So a wall wanted a count. It was the first thing that such a tool asked of whoever meant to use it: how many are behind? A captain who did not know that figure before the thing went up would learn it afterward, by who was left outside.

He had it counted before he was off the moor. There were their five, and the old man made six; there were his own three, and himself; and it made ten.

The scow's bow touched. He watched six people go up the bank toward the lower inn, with a stick behind them, and saw that the stick was no longer a dozen strides back.

---

"It pays nothing," said the bow.

He was on the settle, with the bad leg to the heat, running a thumb along a string that did not want it. He had said the same on a night of snow, and again when the ice went; he was a man who liked a thing said three times before he would leave it alone.

"It pays nothing that I can show you," said Teague.

"Then what's it for? We've the cloth. We've salt work waiting on four holds, and a weir at the mill that wants three stakes yet. I'm not a young man, and that's a week's walk to stand at the edge of a field and watch another crew go into it."

"We'll not be watching."

"We'll not be going in to any purpose, either. You said it yourself, at their hearth. In there I'm a man with a bad hip."

Teague did not answer at once. He owed that man a true answer; the bow had walked behind him for a great many cold terms, and had never once asked to be told a comfortable thing.

"I've been first on that sheet a long while," he said. "It says who brought the goods home. That's all it was ever for, and I've never asked more of it." He looked at the fire. "For the whole of a cold term the only thing in this country worth a man's knowing went on in a shed down that bank, behind a hurdle, with a guard on the door. I was first, and I was on the wrong side of the hurdle. I didn't care for it."

"So it's pride."

"It's that I mean to be at the front of the thing. I'll not dress it. A captain who tells his people that he's going for their sake, when he's going for his own, gets them killed for a lie." He turned his head. "You're not bound. I said my crew comes, and I said it to fifty people. I never said that every man of it must."

The bow snorted, and let the string go.

"I'd like to see you find the mill without me," he said. It was his whole answer, and Teague took it as he would have taken a thumb on a slip.

The old woman had not looked up from her board, where she was cutting linen into lengths with a small knife.

"There's a valley up the north water that breaks its legs every dry spell," she said. "It's done so without me before now. I'm coming for that wrist. I'll not be counted, and I'll not be told where to stand."

"I'd not have counted you."

"See that the others don't."

The boy came with the cloth.

There was no frost to speak of; the thaw was three weeks old, and the joint had not talked since the board came off. He brought it all the same, wrung out of the kettle, on his two palms, as he had on every night since he joined. Teague held out the arm; he had tried to tell him once that it was not wanted, and had seen the boy's face, and had let it be wanted.

"Ten," the boy said. He had been listening on the stair, as he listened to everything. "You said ten, to him. Their six, and us."

"I did."

"Is that four of us?" He had gone red. "Or is it three, and me carrying?"

Teague looked at him, and he was all wrists still. He had grown the width of two fingers since the snow, and had learned from a girl on a bench to wait for a pole; but he went at each thing a half-beat before he had seen the whole of it, and nobody had yet got that out of him.

"You've two legs, and a Path, and you do what you're told a little too soon," he said. "That's a man. I can count as far as four."

The boy nodded, with his whole head, like a horse; and he went away to the stair very quickly, so that nobody should see what his face was doing.

Teague laced the leather, and stood up, and went over his terms once more, as he went over a load before the ropes were drawn.

There were ten on the count, and neither healer; a healer who is counted is a healer who is sent. Whatever the thing paid was to be shared by that count, and so was whatever it cost. The field was his. When it came to steel on a road there would be one voice, and he had heard the boy admit, with his feet, in a square of snow, whose voice it ought to be. And the reading was the pale one's. He could read a slip, and a ledger, and a man's hands; he could not read a calfskin with four rings on it, and he would not stand at her shoulder pretending.

There was one thing more, and it was the name.

He had seen two crews lie up together for a cold term and come out of it as one. He had seen them bury each other's dead as kin. It was a fine thing, and it was how a captain came to turn back for a man who was not his, on a ledge, with his own people strung out behind him. He had four, and he meant to walk four home. He would call the thing by the smallest word that would hold it, and he would not let anybody make it larger.

He put his thumbs in his belt, and went down the bank.

---

The long room of the lower inn was as full as Cael had ever seen it, and Pike had the bench under the cloth.

Every crew that had lain up for the cold was there, and a good many carters. The hide-woman had a stool by the hearth, and the three grey men had their board on a keg and were not throwing. It was the hour at which a one-armed man read out what the day had brought to his plank; and the room had gone quiet for it, as it had on a night of first snow, when a crew came in with white on its shoulders.

Brom did not wait to be called. He went down between the benches with his box still on his shoulder, since he could not get it off without help, and laid a strip of bark on the open ledger.

"That's yours," he said. "To draw the tack on."

Pike took it up, and held it to the candle in his double fashion; a thumb had been set to its foot, in grease, and had left a mark like a crook.

"The shoulder hold. I had a carter in here ten days since, with salt money, who said that he'd come down that cut with his wheels in running water and never a finger lit." He dipped his pen. "I don't write on a carter's word. I write on a thumb."

He wrote, and said it over, twice, to the room.

"*Clearance; the cut below the shoulder hold. Eight. Twenty; paid.* That's twelve lines." The stone went back on the leaf. "And then you never came home. You left word with me: the cut, and whatever lay beyond. A crew's been gone eighteen days past its slip, and I've a line to rule for it, and I don't know what to put. Where's beyond?"

"Treswick," said Cael.

He felt the room take that. It was a registry town, the last on the Line, with three walls and a book at its gate; and there was not a crew on that cloth which had not come east to be out of reach of such places. Somebody set a cup down.

"What'd you do there?"

"We read a survey," said Karis. She stood at Cael's shoulder, with the flat under her arm. "An office has measured four places beyond this river for six years. I had its sheets, and I could not read them; and there was a man there who could show me how such a thing is put together. I have read them now."

"What'd it pay?"

"Nothing."

"And what came of it?"

"A bearing," said Cael.

Pike considered him. Then the pen went into the horn, and he wrote, slowly, in the square black hand; and it was only as the third word went down that Cael understood where he himself was standing.

He had not gone to the front of that bench. The room had pressed in behind Brom, and he had been carried round the bench's end with the press, and had fetched up at the one-armed man's elbow, on the side where the candle was. He was looking down at the leaf over the stump of an arm.

*Treswick. Survey read. Bearing.*

It was the right way up.

He had read every line of that book across twelve feet of tarred oak, with its head toward the man who kept it. It had been turned for him, once, in front of fifty people, and nobody had turned it tonight. He had only walked round a bench, without thinking, to where the writing faced him; and it had not once come into his head to wonder whether he was allowed.

"You're on my side," said Pike, without looking up.

"I'll go round."

"Stop where you are. It reads the same from there. I never said that it didn't." The pen had gone on to the two ruled columns at the line's end, where a price stood, and what became of it. It hung over the first, and passed on, and hung over the second. "I've nothing for those. It paid nothing, and it's not out yet. They'll stand empty, and I'll say that they're empty."

He said so, to the room. Then he turned back three leaves.

It was the season's line, with its ruled space at the end. Cael had seen that space left blank on purpose at first snow, and carried blank over a thaw onto a new cloth.

"I said that I'd leave that until I'd a column for it," said Pike. "I've not found the column. I've had a hundred and sixty-eight days to look." He dipped. "A blank's a kind of entry, so long as a man knows why it's there. But a blank with nothing against it can't be told from one that was forgotten. So I'll put a day."

He wrote it in the space, small: the day, by his own book's reckoning, and no figure after it.

"That's when I left off looking," he said. "It's not a number. It says that on this day the keeper of this book knew what he didn't know, and signed for it." He blotted the place with the heel of his hand. "Eleventh. And dated."

---

Teague came down between the benches while the ink was wet.

He did not hurry; he stopped a stride from the bench's end, with his thumbs in his belt over the hand-axe, as he had stopped at that hearth on a night of snow; and the room, which had begun to talk, left off again.

"I said after the thaw. It's three weeks gone. I said whatever's on that bearing, and I've just heard him write the word." His eyes went along them, and they did not stop at five; they went on to the door-post, where a stick was grounded. "I'll say it here, where it's heard."

[[TIER-B: "Ten of us on a wall's count. Your six and my four." … "Contract. Not more than that. I'll not have it called more." — elided between its two parts; the plan pins the term on Teague and gives the heads (ten on a wall's count; shares by count; the field Teague's; the reading Karis's) without their wording. Staged as Teague's, said to Cael at the bench's end before the whole room, with his eyes having just gone to Vastin at the door-post. The four heads follow below in my own words, as his, so that the restored unit may stand before them or round them — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Cael heard the count before he heard anything else, and so did the room.

There had been five names on that cloth for the whole of a cold term and more. A captain had just said six of them, aloud, and nobody at the bench had corrected him; it was a count for a contract. It was not an admission, and it had not been made by any of the five; and Cael, who did not turn his head, knew by the stillness at the door-post that an old man had heard exactly how much it was and how much it was not.

"Say what it holds," said Brom. "I'll not put my thumb to a word."

"It holds four things. The count's the first, and you've had it. Neither healer's on it. A healer goes where she's wanted, and I'll not have one sent." Teague drew a thumb out of his belt. "Shares by the count. Whatever it pays is cut ten ways; and what it costs is carried the same, so far as a cost can be."

"It's paid nothing yet."

"I know what it's paid. My bow's told me three times." He drew the other thumb. "The field's mine. On a road, with steel out, there's one voice, and it's the one that's had the most practice. I'd not ask it if I thought that you'd say otherwise."

It was said to Cael, and it was said level.

He priced it, as he had been taught to price a thing by a big man who would not let a figure go unsaid. He had led five people into three places where a Path was worth nothing, and had brought them out; nobody else in that room could say as much. But he had been touched three times in a square of snow without once seeing it come, and the man in front of him had learned more from his boots in those three touches than Cael had learned from a face in a year. On open ground, with a watch to be read and ten people to be put somewhere, there was one of them who was better. Cael had written so in a book. He had not been asked to say it in front of fifty.

"It's yours," he said. "I'd have asked you to take it."

"And the reading's hers." The chin went toward Karis, by the width of a finger. "Where it runs, and what a place is when we're stood in front of it. I'll take that from her as I'd take a ford from a man who'd crossed it. I can't read that skin, and I'll not pretend."

"I shall want to be asked before anybody steps over a thing," said Karis. "I shall not always be quick."

"You'll be asked."

"And him?" said Brom. He had not looked toward the door-post. "You've built your count on a thing that you've never seen stand."

[[TIER-B: "The wall's still the wall." — whole, but the plan pins no speaker. Staged as the answer to Teague's term, at the bench's end, with the talk having just come round to Vastin's wall. It could be Cael's (the contract does not make the wall the field's to place); it could be Teague's own (whatever the thing is called, a wall is what the count is built on); it could be Vastin's, from the door-post. The lines after it are written to follow any of the three — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"There's a month on it," said Cael. "It's a healer's, and it's got twelve days to run. He stood one in the middle of it, against her word, for us, and it very nearly cost him the leg. Nobody stands it up before the month's out. Not for your field, and not for mine."

"I'd not ask a thing with a month on it to be shown for a count." Teague looked once more toward the door-post, and it was the look of a man reading a load that he does not mean to lift yet. "I asked what I needed, of a factor. I know where it stands, and that it stays. That'll do me."

He nodded, by the width of a finger, and it was done, and he had not offered his hand. There was a cloth on a nail behind Pike's head with one name alone on its first line and five in a row on its eleventh; and a contract did not take either of them down. Cael found that he did not wish it to. He would sooner walk east beside a man who was ten places better at a thing, and said so, than beside a friend who had to be reminded which of them the ledger preferred.

He wrote it at the hearth's end, when the room had thinned.

*Two hundred and ninety-seventh day. Lowmarch; the hearth's end, and one on the keeper's settle. Eleven miles, and the ferry. The rope is paid: sixty yards of four, and he would take no fare. Pike has twelve lines, and a thirteenth with nothing in its last two columns. He has put a day against the season. A hundred and twenty-eight. Vastin: eighteen of thirty.*

*Teague's term is taken. Ten on his count, by a contract and nothing else. The field is his. He is still the better at it.*

*Known: Pike had a carter's word for the cut before we were home, and Teague had his terms by heart when he came down the room. I read Pike's line from his own side of the bench.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirty-two.*

---

The cart had gone down in the night.

Cael saw it from the bench under the great ash as soon as there was light to see by, on the two hundred and ninety-eighth day, when the meadow was grey and forty fires were being blown up out of their ashes. It was a salt cart, with a pair of mules, and it had been drawn onto the grass at dusk by a carter who did not know how soft three weeks of thaw had left that corner. It stood now with its near wheels sunk to the axle. Half its sacks lay on the turf beside it. The carter had the mules out and was standing with his cap in his hand.

He was standing before Teague.

They were too far off to be heard: twenty strides, across the square of the old silent yard, where the pegs still stood. Brom sat at Cael's side with his arms hanging. They watched a captain look at a cart for some time, as he looked at any load, and then at a lean old woman who had come down the bank with her board under her arm. She said one thing to him, and he answered it; and she shut her mouth, and stood back.

"He's going to do something," said Brom.

Teague walked round the cart's tail. The ash had put a root out there, as thick as a man's thigh, a pace behind the sunk wheel. He set his right heel against it, with care, shifting the boot twice, like a mason bedding a stone. Then he laid his right palm flat on the end of the cart's bed, with the fingers spread.

What he did next took him longer than the thing itself.

He lined himself up; Cael had no better word for it. The heel was set, and the knee came in over the heel, and the hip over the knee; the shoulder dropped, and turned, until the whole of that side of him ran straight from a root in the ground to a palm on a board, like a prop that is set under a beam before the wedges are driven. Not a part of him lay off that line. He stood so for three breaths, and Cael saw the boy move up behind him, on the left, with a cloth steaming across both hands.

There was no light, and there was no sound but the cart's.

It went forward. It did not roll; it was put. The mud let go of two wheels with a noise like a boot drawn out of a bog, and a ton and more of oak and salt went the length of a man's body up onto firm ground, and stopped there, dead, as though a team of six had taken it up at once and then been cut out of the traces.

It went that far and not a hand farther.

Something came back. Cael saw it arrive: the straight arm jarred, from the palm to the shoulder, like a pole that has been struck on its end. And he heard the wrist, at twenty strides, across a meadow: one small dry click, such as a latch makes.

The cloth was round it before the arm had dropped.

"That's a Force," said Brom, very low. "I'd wondered what he kept in that leather. It comes up out of the ground, through a man, and out at the hand; I saw one shift a block at a quarry once, when I was small. They'd sent for him from three valleys off." He was watching the old woman, who had the arm on her board already. "They said that he'd been a deal stronger at twenty."

The carter had found his tongue, and was pointing down the meadow, to where a second cart stood canted in the same soft ground.

[[TIER-B: "Don't put it in front of anything that needs doing twice." — whole, but the plan pins no speaker; it is listed under the contract with the word "Force". Staged in the meadow a breath after the one use, with the arm already in the young one's cloth, when the carter points to a second cart sunk twenty yards off: as Teague's answer, and loud enough to carry to the bench. Teague could own it (to the carter, or across the yard to Cael, who has watched); the lines that follow do not depend on which — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Then the tall man and the bow went down the meadow with three iron bars and a baulk of timber, and the second cart was shifted as a carter would have shifted it, in the better part of an hour.

Cael had the book on his knee before the first bar was set.

Nothing had come to him, and he had not looked for it. He was twenty strides off, on a bench, and nobody in that meadow had meant anything at anybody; a man had put his hand to a cart. But he had seen a line drawn through a body, plainer than he had ever seen one drawn on a hide, and he knew better than to trust it to his memory.

*Seen, at first light, the meadow, from twenty strides. Teague's is Force. He set a heel against a root and a palm against a cart's tail, and stood until every joint between the two lay straight. Then it went: one push, the length of a body, and no more of it after. It wants firm ground behind and a thing in front. What comes back comes down the same road and is paid where it left him. The wrist. I heard it.*

*One use, and the arm is on a healer's board. He would not do a second. Nothing came. Twelve.*

He did not know, that morning, where he would next see that line drawn, or what it would be drawn at.

---

"File," said Teague.

He had the leather on again, over a turn of the old woman's linen, and he carried the arm a little away from his side. He stood under the ash with eight people before him, in the square where they had once hit one another with sticks.

"I'll not drill you. There's no time, and you've your way and I've mine. But ten people who've never walked a road together will walk it like two crews, and a thing that hunts will count two. So we'll walk this meadow, round, till it's one. Nobody speaks. Nobody lights a finger. I'm told that there are hands."

"There are four," said Lira.

She showed them, as she had shown them to an old man on a moor. A flat hand, palm down, was *stand*. A shut fist was *seen*. Two fingers lifted were *come on*, and a hand turned over empty was *nothing*.

The tall man made each of them once, with a big chapped hand, exactly, and grounded his spear. He had not spoken to any of the six in all the days that Cael had known him, and it appeared that four more words which were not spoken suited him very well. The bow made them from the settle of his hip, sourly; and the boy, who had them at the first showing, made them again, quickly, to himself, and then a third time behind his back.

"You're early on the flat one," said Lira. "It's not a race. You go still when you've seen it, not when you think I'm about to."

"I saw your shoulder go."

"Then you stood for my shoulder. Somebody else'll have a different shoulder." She did not smile; she had been taught by a healer how a thing was told. "Wait for the hand."

He nodded with his whole head.

Teague set the file as Cael would have laid out a bout, from the ground upward, and he gave a reason for each place.

"The quiet one's out front, where he's always been. I'll not move him. I couldn't find him to move." It was said without a smile, and Seln, at the north-west peg, inclined his head by a hair. "Then my spear. Then me; and the one with the book at my left hand, a stride back, since he sees a thing before he knows that he's seen it, and I want to be told. Then the girl, and the boy behind her. He'll watch her feet."

"And the papers?" said Brom. "She's at a table, and she'll not be fetched from it."

"Then she'll be told. In the middle, with whoever's healing, and a beast when there's a beast. Then the big one, since he's slow to answer and hard to hurt." His eyes came to the end of the row, where an old man stood on a stick. "Then you. A stride behind him. I'm told that's where you've stood."

"It is," said Vastin.

"And my bow last, where he can see all our backs and the road behind them."

It was the first time that those two had exchanged a word, and Cael marked it. The old man had opened his mouth afterward, as he opened it after any instruction, and Cael waited for the clause; but Teague had already turned to the pegs, and whatever had been prepared was not called for.

They walked the meadow.

They went round it in file, at a road's pace, between the fires. Carters stopped with their bowls half lifted to watch nine people go by without a word. It went badly at first: the two halves of the file kept a different distance, and at the first flat hand the front stood like posts while the back was still closing up. At the first fist the boy looked, and the bow did not, since he had never in his life been told where to look by a girl's hand.

By the third round they stood together.

It was a small thing. Cael, at a captain's left shoulder, a stride back, felt it happen behind him, as a person feels a door shut somewhere in his own house: nine people stopped on one flat hand, and there was not a sound in that corner of the meadow but the fires. He glanced once to his right, and Teague had not turned his head. But the chin had come up, by the width of a finger, at nothing at all.

They were in the fourth when Lira left the file. A small clear voice had called her name from the gate of the inn yard, twice, in a tone that Cael knew. She was gone for as long as it takes to cross a yard, and she came back at a run, and touched his sleeve.

Her face had a look on it that he had last seen at a parapet, above a town, when she priced the whole of a hill in one breath.

"She wants you," said Lira. "Now. She's ruled three boxes on a sheet, and I don't think that she's drawn breath since the second."

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-45.md

# Chapter 45 — Oldest Last

She had the store behind the chimney, where five people had lain out a cold term, and there was not a hand's breadth of the deal that was not covered.

Cael stopped in the door. It was a little past sunrise on the two hundred and ninety-eighth day, and the one small window laid a square of thin light across the whole of it. The courier's flat stood open at the table's head, with its six rows out and a pebble on each. The calfskin lay unrolled beneath them, under the Ternhall brass, and a dead man's book was open at its first page. Three of the Stair's sheets had been taken from their box and set in a row; and in the middle of everything, side by side, lay a leaf of her notebook and a square of stiff card with a turn of brown tape beside it.

Karis stood over the two with her gloves off.

"Shut that," she said, without turning. "And come round to this side. I will not have it read wrong way up."

He came round. Lira had the door, and Brom filled it a moment after, with a quiet man at his elbow who had not been seen to cross the yard.

"I have it," said Karis. "I had it at first light, by the candle, and I have spent an hour and a half since trying to break it. I have not been able to, and I should like six people to try."

She put the butt of her pencil on the card.

"This is his drawing of the fourth line. He drew what was there, and where he could tell a stroke of the writer's from a scratch of the knife's, he has ticked it. This is mine, made the same afternoon with a sewn hand." The pencil crossed to the leaf. "I was sure of three signs in the last six, and I told him so. In that gallery, by one lamp, with the skin before me, it was the truth."

"And by a window?" said Cael.

"By a window I have two copies made by two people who see in two different ways, and I can lay the one upon the other. Where his tick stands and my stroke stands, in the same place, that is a stroke. It gives me the second sign." She drew breath. "Four, of six. And a word of six signs of which I have the first, the second, the fourth and the last is a word that I have met. I kept it under a glove for three days."

She had turned to the Stair's sheets. Her finger went to a group of signs on the nearest; and to the same group on the next, lower down; and to the third.

"It stands four times on that floor, always behind a bearer. I uncovered it on my ninth day under the town, and it was not the word that I had wished for. It was only *set*, as a stone is set, or a stake."

Nobody spoke. Vastin had come to the door by then, on the stick, with the tarred case under his arm; he had stopped on the sill as a man stops who is not sure of a room.

"So the fourth line says this," said Karis. "It says: the mark. And hung from the mark, level, with neither above the other, two things that take it. The one is those who are kindled, and the other is what is set. And after both, a number, which is three."

"What's set?" said Lira. "Set where?"

"I do not know where; the list does not say, and I shall not say it for the list." She held out her bound hand toward the door, palm upward, without looking. "I should like the case."

The old man brought it to the table himself, and undid the strap.

She drew the iron out. It was a stake as long as her forearm, numbered thirty-two, with its point still bright, since nobody had ever driven it; and she laid its head under the window, beside the first page of a dead man's book and the corner of an office's sheet.

A short stroke had been struck into the iron, and beneath the stroke a bar. The same stood in pencil in the book, and in a clerk's ink in the sheet's ruled square.

"That is a thing which was made to be set in the ground," said Karis. "It has a mark on it, which I turned over for a hundred and eighty-three days, and called two copies of one ignorance. I had the word for it all the time. I had read it aloud, once, in another line, and it had not occurred to me that a hole in a moor and a child of fourteen could be given a thing out of the same drawer."

[[TIER-B: "A mark is a grade. The one they give a boy at fourteen says what he's *for*." … "The one on a sheet says how *old* the thing is." — elided between its halves, and the plan pins no speaker (the packet says only that Cleon's list puts the kindling mark and the sheet mark in one line). Staged as Karis's, at the table, with stake thirty-two, the dead surveyor's first page and the Stair's abstract laid together under the window, to the six and Vastin. The first half can stand here. The second half ("how old") is earned on the page only in the next scene, by the running numbers; if the unit must stand whole in one place, it can be moved down to follow "Two hundred and six." without touching either scene — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Cael looked at the three marks, in iron and lead and ink.

He had been stood on a plate once; and something had been written about him at the head of a sheet, in a place that a printer had ruled for it, and he had carried it ever since. He had known at a spring that a hole in the ground could be filed in the same fashion. He had not known until this morning that the two filings were one word, in one line, on the back of a skin.

He put it where he put the rest. It had its verb already, and the verb was hers.

---

"It says three," said Karis. "I have only ever seen one."

She had a clean sheet before her already; she laid the brass along it, held the strip down with the heel of her bound hand, which she was not to lean on, and ruled three boxes side by side.

"There is a stroke with a bar. That is on the stake, and in the book, and on every sheet of the four places that I matched to the hide." She drew it in the middle box, small. "I told you at the spring that there were other sheets, under other signs, and that I would not call them places. I turned their corners down, and I did not tell you everything that I had seen on them."

"You said nothing of their squares," said Seln, from the sill. "I remarked it."

"I thought that you had; I saw the square, and I shut my mouth on it. A second mark, on sheets that I would not so much as call a place, was the first step of a theory; and I had six people round a rock who would have taken the second step for me." She had drawn the sheets out while she spoke, from under six turned corners, one from each row. "They are one group: the same four signs on all six, one sheet to a year. And this is what their square holds."

It was a stroke, and beneath the stroke two bars.

She drew that in the right-hand box; and then she laid her pencil down on the left-hand one, which was empty.

"I have been through all six rows this morning, by the squares alone. There is no sheet with a stroke and nothing under it. I have ruled a box for it because the list says three, and because a thing with one bar and a thing with two want something to stand before them. Nothing goes in it, and I shall not guess what would."

"So there's a kind that there aren't any of," said Brom.

"There is a box that the office has not used in six years. That is all that I will sign."

"It's tiers," said Lira. She had come to the table's corner, and she was looking at the three ruled squares as she had looked at three walls from a rise. "First, second, third. They've a ladder for holes in the ground."

"It is three marks. Call them what you like; I must call them something, and I shall call them so." Karis took the pencil up. "But a grade grades something. The one that is given at a kindling grades a person, and there is a man in this room who signed for a good many." "This one is not for size, as I showed you at the spring; a place of twice the measure has the same bar. It is not for which is fenced. So I have gone through everything else in which one of these sheets differs from another."

She turned the Stair's newest sheet so that its foot lay toward them, and set her finger in the right-hand corner.

There was a number there, small, after a clerk's flourish; Cael had seen it at the spring, on every sheet, and had taken no more notice of it than of the printer's name.

"Ninety-four," said Karis. "On the row beneath it, the same place has ninety-three, and then ninety-two; it goes down by one with every row, to eighty-nine. I took it for a drawer's number, and so it is. I never asked what the drawer was counting."

"It is a series number," said Vastin.

He had not moved from the table's end; he spoke to the sheet, low, and with great care, as a man walks across ice that has let him through once.

"I can tell you what the form is, which is very nearly the only thing that I am good for. Where an office makes a return of the same matter in every year, each sheet is numbered from the first that the office holds. The number is carried forward by whoever draws the next, and nobody is obliged to ask where it began." He stopped. "I have signed a great many returns with such a figure in the corner. I never once read it. I say what the form is; I do not say who drew the first."

"Thank you. That is exactly as much as I wanted." Her finger had gone to the hide, and it touched each ring in turn, going east. "The Fallow's newest sheet is its sixty-first, and the Stair's is its ninety-fourth; the hall's is a hundred and thirty-eight. And the place behind the iron—"

She laid the sheet down upon the fourth ring.

"Two hundred and six."

Nobody at that table said anything at all.

Cael did the sum, since it was there to be done, and it was not a hard one. A sheet a year. Somebody had measured the place behind that fence, from eight points, with a cord, and written that the instruments read true and that the anomaly was total; and somebody had done so in every year for longer than any registry clerk now living had been alive, or his father, or his father's father. Six rows of it lay on this table, and the others stood in a drawer.

"I had supposed that it was six years," said Seln, very quietly. "I read the rows as the whole of the matter. They were only as much of it as had been kept from the people who ought to have received it."

"And the two bars?" said Lira.

Karis turned up the corner of the sixth sheet.

"It has no figure; there are two signs there, out of the index, which I cannot read." She looked down the table. "Is that the form?"

"A series that was opened before the present manner of numbering is carried under its old style," said Vastin. "I should expect it, and I do not know when the manner changed."

"Then I can say that it is older than two hundred and six, and I can say nothing more. I shall not put a figure to it until I have one."

---

There was a man in the door.

Cael had not heard him come, and did not know how long he had stood there. He had his thumbs in his belt, and his right arm hung a little away from his side, in its leather. He had not come in, and he had not so much as looked at three ruled boxes; his eyes were on the calfskin, and on a strip of brass that lay along its pencil line and ran off the edge of the skin.

"I'm not here for the marks," said Teague. "I'd not know one from another; I want to know how far."

"I was coming to that," said Karis. She did not seem put out. "The sheets with two bars have one line that the others lack, above their eight stations. It gives the four signs of the place behind the iron, and then a point of the compass, and then a measure in lengths. I saw that at the spring as well, and took it for a fence's survey tied to its own floor." Her pencil tapped the line. "It is a run, from the fourth place to this one. An office has written down how far it is from the one to the other, and which way."

"Which way?"

"By a point of the compass, which I shall lay against the hide before I say it aloud." She held out her right hand, across the table, toward Cael. "You have a pair of brass legs in your coat. I asked for the loan of them when I should have a hide in front of me again."

He had known that it was coming since she said *run*.

He took out Hesk's book, and the dividers slid from between its leaves into his palm, shut, with the cork upon their points. He had carried them for six days, under a ruling of his own that was a hundred and thirty days old: he did not put his hand to a thing that measured, since a cord had run short in it and two edges had disagreed. An old man in Denvash had sent these to step off a line with, and had written that lines were for finding out how far a thing is from another thing.

Cael laid them on the deal, and pushed them across with one finger.

"They're lent," he said. "I'll not open them."

"I had not asked you to."

She drew off the cork, and opened the legs, with her right hand, upon the hide; it was the first time that they had been opened on a distance by anybody. One point went down in the middle of the hall's ring, and the other in the middle of the fourth.

"The carter who drew this skin drew it in days. Brom. The hall to the iron."

"Three," said Brom. "With a mule, and a girl in a sling, and a healer gone north."

"A surveyor's length I know to the stride. I walked behind that cord for a whole cold term, and I know what a day of ours comes to in it." Her lips moved over the office's figure. "This run is three such days, less a little." She closed the legs by the width of a hair; then she lifted one point, and swung it round, along the brass, as a person walks a pair of compasses across a chart.

It came down off the skin.

It stood on the bare deal, a hand's breadth beyond the calfskin's edge, hard against the Ternhall strip; and the low sun from the window laid the brass's shadow over it, so that the point stood in a narrow dark line.

"There," said Karis. "I have laid the office's point against the carter's line, and they do not differ by the width of this strip. It is under the edge, and I did not put it there. I laid a figure on a hide, and that is where it fell."

"Three days past the iron," said Teague.

"As we walk. A little less."

Cael looked along the skin, from the left hand to the right. There were four rings, and a point that was not yet drawn, and a number against each: sixty-one, and ninety-four, and a hundred and thirty-eight, and two hundred and six. They rose as they went. Beyond the last of them, in a strip of shadow, stood something that the office had stopped counting.

[[TIER-B: "Somebody laid them down in order" — a fragment (no stop given), and the plan pins no speaker. Staged at the table, with the four running numbers read along the hide from west to east and the divider's point standing past the fourth ring: the first thing said aloud once all five are in view. Brom could own it, pricing what he sees; so could Lira, or Cael; Karis could own it only as a thing that the paper shows. The next lines follow from any of them — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"Who?" said Lira.

It was the question, which had been asked on a road once, of a pattern, and had not been answered. The old man at the table's end had drawn breath, and Cael saw the right hand leave the stick, and begin to rise.

[[TIER-B: "It's observation. I'm telling you what the paper says. Not what it means." — whole, but the plan pins no speaker. Staged as Karis's, at the table, to Lira's "Who?" and to Vastin's drawn breath, with her bound hand flat on the sheet of three boxes. If it is another's (Cael's, said for her), the lines on either side still stand — coordinator to supply protected text]]

The hand came down upon the ash.

"Noted," said Vastin.

It was one word. He had not begged anybody's pardon, or said that he was drafting. He had made a stroke at the foot of the thing, as a person does who has heard a term and means to keep it; and then he looked up the table at the boy whose word it was, with something in the grey face that was very nearly surprise at himself.

Lira had turned her head. So had Brom.

"That's ours," she said, under her breath.

"He's had it by him long enough," said Brom.

The man in the door had not followed any of that; he had been looking at the hide the whole while, as he looked at a load.

[[TIER-B: Teague: "Oldest last." … "Then that's where we're going." — elided between its two parts; the plan pins Teague. Staged in the doorway of the back room, with his eyes on the hide and the divider's point, to nobody in particular and then to Cael. Whatever stands between the two parts may be his or another's (a question from Cael, or Karis's assent); the scene ends on the second part — coordinator to supply protected text]]

---

They were on the south road by the middle of the forenoon, in the file that a captain had set under an ash.

It held. Cael had not been sure that it would, outside a meadow. A quiet man went ahead, small against the pale grass, and after him seven feet of ash with a leaf of steel on it. Then came a pair of shoulders that did not look round; and Cael walked a stride behind the left of those, where he had been put. He had never in his life walked at anybody's shoulder to be asked what he saw.

Behind him he could hear the rest by their feet. There was a quick tread and a quicker one after it, which kept changing its length. There was Karis's, with the flat and the hide, and beside it the tap of a lean old woman's staff. A big man came next with a box; and last of all there was a slow uneven step, the bad hip of a man with a crossbow on his back.

There was no stick in it.

That had been settled in the yard, and it was the first thing that the field had cost anybody. The salt cart that had been put out of the mud was going down to the mill for meal, light, behind its two mules; and its carter had said to Teague, cap in hand, that a man's arm was owed something. Teague had looked at an old man with a slat on his leg, and at the length of a ridge.

"It's owed a seat," he had said. "For him. I'll not walk ten at the pace of one."

The old man had opened his mouth. Then he had looked at the nine who would have had to wait for him, and had shut it, and gone to the tail-board. He had offered his copper, and the carter would not have it; he said that it was the captain's fare, and paid.

So a cart went on a furlong ahead, with a brown coat on its tail, and for the fourth day together Cael walked toward a pair of boots that hung.

They passed the well at the fork, which had a house over it now, with a door hung and a new rope on the windlass; a crew had taken that from the plank in the cold. He put his hand on the curb as he went by, since he had hauled foul air out of that shaft for three days on a bitten leg, a long while ago. Then the road climbed, and they were on the back of the ridge.

It was the same pale grass, combed all one way, and the same wind. The country fell off on both hands. He had come along it once in the dark before a frost, behind a mule, to ask a healer whether she would come; and he had gone back along it with the answer. He had not known then that the left-hand ruts ran to anything but three holes in the ground.

Nobody spoke. Twice a flat hand went up, far ahead, for nothing that Cael ever saw, and the file stood, and went on at two fingers. The second time the boy stood exactly when Lira did.

They came to the fork a little after noon, where one pair of ruts kept the ridge. The other went down the western face through a notch that wheels had worn, and the cart had stopped at its head, where the carter was locking a wheel. The old man had got down there, as he got down for every hill.

There was a flat rock there. Teague lifted his chin at it, and the file broke, and sat, and the old woman undid a strap of leather and looked at a wrist.

Cael took out the book.

He had sat on this rock with it once, and written two lines about a healer. The mill's wheel was to be heard below, very small, like a tired man splitting wood. He set the day at the head, and the count.

*Two hundred and ninety-eighth day. The fork above Millrace; the flat rock. A hundred and twenty-eight. Vastin: nineteen of thirty. He rides, on the field's ruling. Karis opened the dividers. I lent them.*

Then he wrote the rest as it came, and it came all in one piece.

*Karis cracked the grade. The sites are ranked by age and the bearing runs oldest-last — somebody laid them out in order, and the order runs into the deep edge, toward the first one they ever made. Second-tier's the fourth site, the one the Compact fenced. Third-tier's out past it, on the line, quiet for centuries. Teague's crew came after the thaw like he said — ten of us now, and he's still better at this than I am, and the ledger still says so. Two crews, one bearing, and a straightedge my guardian sent me because my grandmother said a man who catalogues everything eventually needs to draw a line through it. Turns out she was right about the line. Turns out the line was already drawn four hundred years ago. We're just the first to follow it to the end.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirty-two.*

He read it through, and it was more than she would have let him say at a table. He had not said it at a table; he had put it where he put what he thought, with his own name to it, and he let it stand.

A hand came over his shoulder; he gave Lira the book, and she read it with her lips.

"Your grandmother," she said.

"Hesk's wife, and I never knew her. She's only ever been a sentence on an order-leaf."

"It's a good one." She read the last lines again. "You've put a figure on it."

"I've put what I think. Karis has a box with nothing in it; I'm allowed a page."

She shut the book and handed it back, and looked down the notch, where a wheel was knocking.

---

The valley was green, and the water came down it brown and fast over a weir of stakes and stone.

Three of the stakes were gone from the weir. Cael saw the gap as they came round the shoulder, and saw a captain see it; that had been a slip on Pike's plank once, at four marks, and it had been forfeited for a walk along a ridge. The tall grey building stood beyond, with its wheel going round in a white fall. There was the fence of split posts, and the dozen roofs, and the ford below with a flat stone on either bank.

A woman sat on the nearer stone with a net across her knees.

She raised her head at the sound of boots, and counted them down the track with her chin, one at a time, and did not hurry over it. Then she turned and called toward the mill, without getting up, in a voice that had been pitched to carry over a wheel.

"Eleven off the ridge."

The answer came from a lean-to against the mill's southern wall, low and hoarse, from somebody who had not troubled to come to the door.

"I know."

There was a brown mule tied to a fence-post, with a white foot. She had her head up already, and both ears pricked at a big man with a box; and Brom, who could not lift an arm to her, stopped in the track and said something under his breath that was not meant for anybody else.

The bench along the mill's wall was empty. It was the end of a day, and whoever had sat there since first light had been seen and sent home. The old man had come down the notch last, a tread at a time, behind the locked wheel; he reached the bench as the others reached the fence, and looked at it, and plainly thought it wiser to be found on his feet.

Oryn came out of the lean-to, wiping her hands a finger at a time.

She was thinner. That was the first thing, and Cael had not expected it; three weeks of five gates had come off her as a cold term comes off a horse. She stopped at her stride and a half and looked the eleven of them over, and it took her no longer than it had in a cut.

Her eyes passed over him as they passed over a gatepost. They went to a girl who stood with both hands on her hips; to a small pale woman with one of hers inside her coat; to a big man whose arms hung. They went to four people whom she had seen across a meadow, and never had her hands in. And they stopped on a leg that was twice the size it had been when she bound it.

Her mouth went as it went at the smell of a wound left too long.

The lean old woman had come up along the wall, with her staff and her board. She stood a pace from the healer's elbow, and neither of them said a word. They looked at one another once, for the length of a breath, as two carpenters look who have each been shown the other's joint; and then both of them, together, turned and looked at Brom's right shoulder.

"I'll come to that," said Oryn. "It's had a box on it; I can see the lean from here." She had not taken her eyes from the big man. "And I'll come to you," she said, in the same voice, to the old one by the bench. "Stand there."

It was a stride to his left, by the lean-to's post, where the light was.

Cael watched him. He had heard that man answer every instruction that was ever put to him, for three hundred miles: with *I cannot undertake*, with *as to the last*, with a clause hung on the end like a seal on a ribbon. He saw the mouth open.

It shut again. The stick moved, and the leg after it, and the old man stood where he had been told, and said nothing whatever.

[[TIER-B: "Nineteen days." / "Then it's eleven more." — the two lines are whole, but the plan pins neither speaker. Staged at the lean-to's post, with Vastin standing where he has just been ordered to stand and Oryn looking at the slat from her stride and a half; one of the two gives the count and the other the remainder. Vastin could own the first (he has called his own day every morning since the cut) and Oryn the second; or the other way about. Nothing after this depends on which — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"There's a count," said Teague.

He had come down to the fence with his thumbs in his belt. It was the first time that he had spoken to her; in her ten days at Lowmarch she had not once gone up the bank.

"Ten on it, for a line that runs east. It's contract, and it's mine in the field, and neither of you is on it." His chin went to the old woman. "She's said that she'll not be counted. I'd sooner hear yours from you than be told it by a boy."

Oryn looked at him as she looked at a patient who has walked in on a broken foot and wishes to discuss the weather.

[[TIER-B: Oryn's terms restated — the packet gives only two fragments, "three holds owed" and "a stop on my own route"; its sense is given too (she comes east as a healer on her own round, and is on nobody's count). Staged as Oryn's, at the fence, in answer to Teague, with the six and Teague's four in hearing. Her own sentences are not written here — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Nobody argued it. Brom had gone into the mill by then, and he came out with the miller's wife behind him and a sack on the bound shoulder; it was a mark's worth of meal and a flitch, for five days of country with no gate in it. He said the figure as he came: a hundred and twenty-seven.

Then he set down what he had carried under his box's lashing for seventeen days, which was a wooden bowl, on the end of the bench.

[[TIER-B: "Sixth chair." / "On my terms. Which are the only kind." — the second line is Oryn's (Tier A 25 lists it with her Millrace lines); the plan does not pin the speaker of the first. Staged at the mill's bench at dusk, with Brom's bowls laid along it and Oryn's own bowl set at its end before an upturned keg. Lira could own "Sixth chair." (she told Oryn at this mill that there was a bowl); so could Brom, or Cael. Oryn answers, and sits — coordinator to supply protected text]]

She sat.

It was a keg, and not a chair. She lowered herself onto it as she did everything, squarely, and took up the bowl; and Cael, who had watched that woman eat standing for as long as he had known her, a stride and a half from the nearest of them, found that he had to look at the weir for a while.

---

"Shoulder," said Oryn. "The left. May I?"

She had lit a lantern in the lean-to, and hung it from the turf roof's pole. The wheel was still going on the other side of the stone, and Brom sat on the plank across two kegs, with his shirt off, like a boulder that somebody had brought indoors.

"Aye," he said.

The old woman had already gone to the right, and she did not ask; it was her own knot, and nobody else had been let to touch it. She picked at the drover's turn with her nails, and it came, and four yards of webbing went slack across his chest. Oryn watched her do it, and did not put out a hand.

Then her own thick fingers went round the left shoulder, before and behind.

"That's a wrench," she said, with her eyes shut. "It's a fortnight old, near enough, and you've used it since. You took a weight on it that was coming down, and you took it as a man. There's nothing of yours in it. If you'd brought the grey up, I'd be feeling a different thing."

"I'd a clerk's yard all round me; I'd have been a line in a book."

"I'm not blaming you; I'm reading it." Her lips moved. "I can shut that, since it's torn, and a torn thing I can do. Hold still."

It took her a quarter of an hour. When her hands came away she was grey about the mouth, and Brom lifted his left arm slowly, as a man lifts a lid that he expects to be nailed. It went to the height of his chin.

She had moved to the right, where the old woman's hands were.

"And this I said I'd not touch. Twice mended, and not a third. It's had a box on it for twelve days, against what I told you at a fork." Two fingers pressed. "It's no worse. I can't think why."

The old woman said nothing. She took the webbing up again, and laid it shorter this time, in two yards and not four, with the turn behind the arm, and drew it down with a jerk that made the big man grunt. Then she looked at the healer, and Oryn looked at the knot.

"That'll do," said Oryn.

It was as much as either had said to the other, and Cael thought that it was a good deal.

"Arm," she said next. "The left. May I?"

"Yes," said Lira.

She had it out already, bare to the elbow, with the eight marks pale along it. She sat on the plank with her chin up, as she had sat on a block at Thornwater when a knot was undone; and the healer took the forearm in her two hands and went along it from the wrist.

[[TIER-B: "You listened when it told you the second time." / "Once." / "Once is once more than I priced" — the third line is given without its stop, and no speaker is pinned. Staged on the plank, with Oryn's hands on the forearm: Oryn reading what the arm has done since Thornwater, Lira answering in one word, Oryn's reply. That order of speakers is the natural one and is not the plan's — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"It's an arm," said Oryn, and laid it down in the girl's lap.

Lira looked at it there, and shut the hand, and opened it. She did not say anything; but she drew her sleeve down over the eight marks herself, which Cael had not seen her do since the Fallow.

Karis had to be asked twice, since she had brought her notebook to the lean-to's post and was writing in it by the lantern.

"Hand. May I?"

"Yes. I beg your pardon. Yes."

The strip of shirt came off, turn by turn. Under it the palm was laid open to the light: a long straight seam across the heel, from the root of the thumb to the wrist's far side, drawn together with seven stitches of black waxed thread, each as thick as a bootlace's end.

"Who sewed this."

"I did," said Brom. He had gone red. "With a roping needle. There wasn't a healer inside that gate who'd touch her without a name."

Oryn bent closer, and turned the hand to the lantern, and looked along the seam as a person looks along a furrow.

"It's the ugliest piece of sewing that I ever saw hold," she said. "It's held; it's shut from end to end, and it's clean." She had a small knife out. "They've done what they were put in for. I'll have them out, and I'll spend a little where the thumb pulls. You've been writing with it."

"It was required."

"I don't doubt it. Write. Don't lean."

The stitches came out one at a time, and Karis counted them, under her breath, in a tongue that nobody else there had.

That left the post.

He was still standing at it; he had stood there for the better part of an hour, on one leg and a stick, in the lantern's edge; and when she turned to him at last, wiping the knife, she stopped.

"You've not moved."

"I was told to stand there," said Vastin. "I was not told that I might leave off."

Something went across her tired face that was not a smile and was a good deal nearer one than he had ever had of her.

"Sit on the plank. Leg out. I want my hands on that knee, and on your wrist. May I?"

"Yes." He let himself down. "There is a thing which you must have first. I asked that you be told it, eight days ago, and I find that I had rather tell you. I stood a wall upon that leg on the eleventh day, and I had your words in my head as the stick came down. I heard them, and I did it."

"I can see what you did. I've been looking at it since you came down that notch."

Her hands went round the joint, above the slat and below.

[[TIER-B: "It held in a cut" — a fragment, with no speaker pinned. Staged on the plank, with Oryn's hands on the knee and her eyes shut, after Vastin has told her of the wall on the eleventh day. Oryn could own it (reading the joint: what it bore on the road and what it was then asked to bear in a stair); Vastin could own it, of the one time he stood on it with the stick up, two days ago; Cael could, speaking for him. Her verdict follows in my words — coordinator to supply protected text]]

She did not spend on it. She took the slat off, and bound the joint again in her own linen with two new laths; and what she said, when she had counted his wrist, was that the month stood as it had been counted, and that he was not to mistake that for a kindness.

[[TIER-B: "Noted" / "That's new." / "It's a useful word." / "It's his." — four lines, none pinned to a speaker. Staged on the plank as the close of the knee's reading: one of them answers her verdict with the word; she remarks on it; it is defended; and its owner is named. Vastin could own the first and third, Oryn the second, and Vastin or Lira the fourth (with a look toward Cael, who stands at the lean-to's post); the scene ends on it — coordinator to supply protected text]]

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-08-before-the-paths/manuscript/chapter-46.md

# Chapter 46 — Not Flow

He found her above the weir, at the last of the light, sitting on a stone with her hands in the race.

She had spent on a shoulder and a hand that evening, and had read an arm and a knee; and she sat as she had sat on a road after four holes in a leg, with her head down and her lips moving. He waited until they stopped, while the water came down the cut channel beside her, fast and brown, toward a wheel that had not yet been shut off for the night.

"You've a thing to say," said Oryn, without turning. "You've had it in your boots since you came down that notch, and you walked as if one of them had a stone in it."

"Two things. I wrote that you'd have them from me at this mill, before anything else was said."

"Then say them. I've a loaf to eat before I'm fit for you, and I'll hear it while I eat."

He had the book out, and he did not open it; he had found on a ridge that he did not need to.

"You told me to write it down and stop touching it. On the two hundred and eighty-first day I read myself twice, with yours: once in a field by a brook, and once at a milestone, in the dusk." He kept it level. "I went round the place, and not into it, and I told myself that round wasn't touching. I knew what you'd meant when you said it. I read your words for the leave that I wanted. I'd do it again, and I'm not asking you to say that it was right."

She had taken her hands out of the water, and she dried them, a finger at a time, on the skirt of a dead man's coat.

"What did you get?"

"The same nothing. But I found out what the nothing was. Yours feels what moves; it was all that it ever felt, in you or in me. Where nothing moves it comes back empty, and it would come back empty from a gatepost. I'd been calling that a reading of the place, and it was only the end of the tool."

"I could have told you that."

"You did tell me. You said that you'd never felt such a thing in anybody, and I heard a finding. It was a limit."

She was silent for a little while, with the loaf in her lap, and then she broke it.

"I set a rule on you for a healer's reason," she said. "A person who's found a sore place goes back to it every night with his fingers. You kept my words, and you went round what they were for, as a carter goes round a toll by way of the field; and that's worse than breaking it outright, since it's what clever people do." She chewed. "And then you carried it seventeen days, and came down a ridge to tell me so before I'd laid a hand on you. That's the other half of it, and I'll take the two together. It's entered. What's the second?"

"There are twelve. You had ten at the fork."

Her jaw stopped.

"The eleventh's the old man's. It came in a stair, under the town, while he held a wall for us on that knee, and he doesn't know. I can't tell him how it came without telling him how all of them came, and that's six people's to give."

"That's a debt."

"It's written as one."

"And the twelfth?"

"There's a man under the registry who reads how a made thing stands: which stone bears, and which is carried. He was showing Karis the inside of it, and I was holding his lamp." He drew breath. "It wasn't taken. It turned round in me, as a sheet is turned on a desk. He's been told, since it was his; he said that it wasn't supposed to be possible, and then he poured me a cup of water."

"What does it do?"

"It leans. It'll lean at a table, or a bridge, or a hinge, and I've to stop it looking. It gives one word, and it bills the eyes for an hour afterward. It reads only what somebody made, and he was plain about that. Not a river, and not a tree." He made himself finish. "Not a man."

Oryn had turned on her stone, and she looked at him as she had not yet looked at him that day, the whole of him, from the boots.

"You've a place in you that doesn't move," she said slowly. "And you've been handed a thing that reads what doesn't move."

"Yes."

"Have you turned it there?"

"No. It offered, on the first afternoon, and I said not until you'd heard."

"I've heard." She ate the last of the crust. "I'll tell you what I think, and it's a healer's, as it was before. The man who owns that tool told you what it's not for, and a man generally knows his own tool. That's his word. Here's mine. Tonight I'm going into you as deep as I go, and I can't read a thing that's being stirred while I'm reading it. So for as long as my hands are in you, nothing's turned: not that, and not any of the others."

"And after?"

"After, you're on a road, with your own eyes to spend, and I mend what's brought to me. I don't keep people." She got up, stiffly, with a hand on the stone. "But not on my plank."

---

The miller shut the sluice at dark.

Cael heard it done from the lean-to: a rattle of chain, and a gate going down, and then the great wheel slowing through three turns, each longer than the last, until it stood. The knocking that had gone on under every word since they came into that valley left off. There was only the race, running by to waste, and the small hiss of the lantern on its pole.

She had her things laid out along the shelf, as she had laid them on a sill at Thornwater, and the plank lay across its two kegs. Beyond the lantern's edge a girl sat on the bench's end, against the mill's warm stone, with her knees drawn up. She had not been asked to stay, and it had not come into anybody's head to ask her to go.

"On your back," said Oryn. "Boots off. You'll want them off before I've done, since I'll be a good while."

He lay down, and the wood was as hard as he remembered it.

"You know how it goes, and I'll say it again, since it's the form. You breathe like a man asleep. You don't hold anything shut, and you don't let anything rise, and you don't help me; and if you must move, you say so first." She stood over him with her sleeves pushed up and her thick hands hanging. "I'll talk, since it's how I keep my place. And this time I'm not stopping at twenty minutes. I'll stop when I've been where I'm going, or when there's none of me left to go with."

"How long?"

"I don't know. I've never gone so far into anybody who wasn't dying." She looked down at him. "May I?"

"Yes."

Her left hand slid in under the back of his neck, broad and warm, and took the weight of his head; and her right came down flat upon his breastbone.

He counted breaths, since it was the only count that she had left him. A man asleep draws twelve to the minute, and he began at one.

It came as it had come once before, on a scrubbed table. It did not go down into him, as his own read went down into the ground; it went along. He felt it leave her right hand somewhere about the thirtieth breath, and set out toward his feet without haste, as a joiner runs a thumb down the edge of a plank to learn whether it is true. It turned where there was no more of him, and came up the other side, and arrived under his neck, and set out again. There was the old hitch in it, a shade late upon his right side; he knew that now for a taped heel, favoured so long that it had got into everything she did.

"That's skin," she said, from a long way off. "That's breath. Those are the three that I taped; they've been leaned on since, and they've forgotten it. That's a heart that's had a hard week."

He let them go by, and did what she had taught him on that table, which was nothing at all: every door in the house left standing as it happened to stand. He had thought it then the hardest thing that he had ever been set; and he had done it since on a road, on every day, with ten of them and then with twelve.

"Now yours."

She went among them slowly, and named each as she came to it by what her hands met; and he lay in the dark behind his eyelids and heard himself told off like a load on a landing.

"That one's quick, and it's hardly got a bank; I know whose it was. That's a deep narrow one. That's the heavy one, the workhorse, down in the soles, and here's something that waits, right through the frame. A hot point, by the left hand. A thing with a lid on it, which I'll not lean on; it's lifted since dusk, and you're keeping it down, and I'll let you, since it's that or have it up." She breathed. "Three lanes, like a ploughed field. A fine thin one that wants a corner to sit in. A short sharp one. And mine."

He was at the hundred and fortieth breath, and Tide lay in him as still as she stood, since it knew its owner.

"That's ten, and I had ten. Now where's the old man's?"

He did not answer, since he was not to.

"There. I'd have walked past it. It doesn't run at all; it stands in the stream like a post, and everything parts for it. That's eleven." Her hand had not shifted on his chest. "And there's a little flat thing behind your eyes, laid by, like a rule folded on a shelf. It's doing nothing. That'll be the reader's."

She was silent for six breaths, and he felt the slow current pass down him once more and return.

"Twelve," she said. "I'll not ask how many more you mean to bring me."

---

"Now the middle," said Oryn.

It was the two hundred and sixth breath. He had been on that plank for seventeen minutes, and she had only just finished with what he carried.

He felt her go to it, as she had gone once before. Her current came up his left side toward the hollow under the breastbone; and where it should have passed through, it parted.

He had not had a picture for that until three days ago, and he had one now. He had stood on a bridge and counted piers so that he need not look at them, and he had seen what a brown river does at a cutwater. It does not stop there, and it does not go in. It divides upon the stone, and runs down either side, and joins again below; and a person on the bank can tell the whole shape of the pier by nothing but the water.

"There," she said, very low. "It's as it was. Everything goes about it."

She did not leave it, this time. She had come as far as this before, and had said what she met, and gone out again; tonight she stayed. He felt her send the slow limping current round it on the left hand, and then on the right, and then, which she had never done, round it from above and from beneath. She went about that still place as a person goes about a house which has every shutter closed, trying each wall in turn with the flat of a hand.

"There's no bank," she said. "It's not a bank that stops me. There's nothing there to push on. I come up to it, and then I'm past."

He drew the two hundred and fortieth breath. That was twenty minutes, and she had said that she would not stop at twenty.

"It's not small. I'd thought that it was small."

She went round, and round again. Her voice had sunk to almost nothing. The heel of her hand on his breastbone eased and settled with each passing, and he lay beneath it and kept every door as it stood.

It was at about the three hundredth breath that the twelfth turned.

He had not bidden it, or so much as thought of it, since he had been counting, and the count was all of him that was awake. It had lain where she said, a little flat thing behind his eyes, folded; for seven days it had leaned at whatever a hand had made, at pegs and rivets and the piers of a bridge, and each time he had given his eyes some other work, and it had come up short.

His eyes were shut now, and there was nothing that he could give them to do.

And something was being drawn in him, under her hands, which had never been drawn before. It was an outline. Her current went round the place on every side, and round again; and the thing behind his eyes came about to that, mildly, as a head comes round in a dark room toward the only sound in it.

It did not look outward. It leaned in.

*No*, he began to say; and he found that he could not say it without saying it with the whole of himself. A refusal was a holding, and she would feel a jaw go hard. She had felt it once, at the hundred and eighth breath, on another table, and had told him that he was helping.

He could not shut that door, and he was not to open it, and so he left it as it stood.

It leaned, for the space of three breaths, at the middle of him.

*Still.*

There was no voice in it; it was only there, as the weight of a stone is there in the hand that lifts it. It was not *nothing*. He knew how the thing said that: flatly, of a red square of tile, or of a table's idle leg. It was not *sits*, which it said of a page. It was a word that he had never had of it before; and he had used that very word himself, in a log, for longer than he had owned the thing which now said it.

Her current made one more round, coming up from beneath, and parted, and joined.

And the lean came down flat, as a foot arrives upon a floor.

*Set.*

His heart struck once, hard, against the heel of a healer's hand.

He let the breath out slowly, like a man asleep, to the count, and he drew the next. He had stood on a river bar with a hound's teeth in the sack on his arm, and had not answered it, and that had been easier than this. He kept his jaw loose, and he kept his two hands lying open on the plank, with the palms upward, and did not let one finger of them close.

*Set*: as a stone is set, or a stake. He knew three places where he had met that word. A thing had come into him in a stair, not as a filling, but as a post is stood in its hole; a small pale woman had found it under her own glove, when she had wished for a name; and it stood in the fourth line of a list on the back of a skin, hung level with the people at a kindling.

There was a next word. He could feel the shape of it, as a tongue feels the gap where a tooth has been.

He did not let it come. He put it down, as he had seen an old man put down a sentence in a gallery, with one hand off the stick; and he lay under a healer's palm and went on with his count.

"Something turned over," said Oryn.

It was a murmur, from very far away.

"Not in the middle. Out at the edge of you. One of yours, in its sleep." Her hand had not moved. "Leave it. It's nothing to me."

He left it. He was at three hundred and eleven, and he had never in his life lain so still.

---

She came out of him at the four hundred and ninetieth breath.

He knew it by her hands: the one on his breastbone lifted first, and then she drew the other from under his neck, a little at a time, and let his head down upon the wood. She stood for a moment with both of them hanging; then she felt behind her for the keg, and sat on it heavily, and put her elbows on her knees.

He had seen her grey, and he had not seen her so. Her face in the lantern was the colour of the ash in a dead ring, and her cropped head hung, and her lips went to fifty, and began again at one.

Lira had come off the bench. She stood at the lean-to's post with a cup of water from the race, and she did not offer it until the second count was done.

"That's forty minutes," said Oryn at last, and drank. "I said that I'd go as deep as I went, and that's it. There's a floor to me, and I've had both feet on it this quarter-hour."

Cael had sat up. The lantern had begun to hurt him a little at the edges, already, and he looked at the turf of the roof instead.

"You'll want it plain," she said. "I've only the one way of giving it."

She set the cup down upon the shelf, among her things.

"There's a place in you I can't read because it isn't *flow*. I've spent my life reading flow. Whatever that is, it's not the kind of thing my Path was built for."

The race ran by in the dark, and nobody said anything.

He had heard her say before that a thing was past her hands. She had said it on a hillside, of a girl's arm; and she had told him afterward that when she said so he was to believe her, and not stand looking at her as though she had kept something back. He believed her. She had gone round that place for the better part of half an hour, at every height, with all that she had, and she had not come out and said that it was nothing. She had said what she was, and where she ended.

It cost her to say it. He saw that, and he thought that it had cost more than the forty minutes. A person who has read every body that was ever laid before her, for thirty rounds of a country, had sat down on a keg in front of a boy and a girl and told them the size of her own tool.

It was a whole finding, and it was hers.

He had another. It lay behind his eyes in two words, and the two findings did not say the same thing. Hers said that there was no flow in that place for a Tide to feel. His said that something stood there which had been put.

He looked at the roof, and kept his own.

It was not the first thing that he had kept back. He had kept a date from everybody, at a spring, and a warning. But he had never before kept a thing from the person whose hands had drawn it out, with those hands hardly cold upon him; and he found that it had a weight which the others had not.

"That's all?" said Lira. She was not asking him.

"It's all that I've a right to. I could dress it for you. I could say that it's a stone, or a knot, or a door; I thought all three on a road north, between one gate and the next, and they're none of them true." Oryn rubbed her eyes with the heel of her hand. "I know what I read, and I know when I'm not reading. I'll not make a guess sound like a finding because a boy's come a long road for one."

"I didn't come for one," said Cael. "I came to hear yours."

"Then you've heard it."

Lira had not moved from the post. She had her chin up, and Cael knew what was coming, since he knew that girl. A thing had been asked for at a fork-stone, in so many words, and she had carried the asking for eighteen days as she would have carried a count.

[[TIER-B: "Is it a finding?" / "It's a leaf …" / "Then it's not mine." … "Tell me on the road, if there's road. Not at my table." … "Lie still when I say. That's still the term." — elided in three places; the plan says only that Lira offers the leaf and that Oryn does not hear it. Staged in the lean-to after the finding: Lira reminds Oryn that she asked at the fork for whatever walked out of the archive, all of it, and says that there is a paper in Cael's coat which did; Oryn asks whether it is a finding; Lira answers; Oryn declines to have it read at her table, and restates her term. The fair copy stays in Cael's coat throughout and is not unfolded. Oryn's last line may belong here or to the first scene of this chapter, where her term is given in my words — coordinator to supply protected text]]

The paper stayed where it was. Cael had put his hand to his coat when the girl began, and he took it away again.

He was not sorry.
